--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Mission analysis:**
- Score decreases by 1 for delivering "good" items (one color half), increases by 1 for delivering "bad" items (other color half)
- We need to: explore to find source, pick up items from the CORRECT half (blue or green - we need to test which is beneficial), return to nest (white floor, red wall), deliver
- Key perceptions: floor color (BLACK=source, WHITE=nest, GRAY=middle), wall colors (RED=nest side, BLUE/GREEN=source halves)

**Strategy:**
Since we don't know which color (blue or green) is beneficial vs. harmful, I'll try machines that:
1. Go toward BLUE source (color 3=blue), return to nest via RED wall (color 1=red)
2. Go toward GREEN source (color 2=green), return to nest
3. Mix of exploration + targeted movement

**State design:**
- State for exploring/going to source
- State for returning to nest
- Transitions based on floor color (BLACK=arrived at source, WHITE=arrived at nest)

Colors: clr 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan
LED colors: 0=off, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan

**Core FSMs:**

FSM1 (2-state, blue-seeking): 
- S0: GoToColor blue (clr=3), transition on BLACK floor → S1
- S1: GoToColor red wall (clr=1), transition on WHITE floor → S0

FSM2 (2-state, green-seeking):
- S0: GoToColor green (clr=2), transition on BLACK floor → S1  
- S1: GoToColor red (clr=1), transition on WHITE floor → S0

FSM3 (3-state with exploration):
- S0: Exploration, transition BLACK → S1, transition WHITE → S2
- S1: GoToColor blue, transition WHITE → S2
- S2: GoToColor red, transition BLACK → S0 (wrong), transition WHITE stays...

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to see the results from Round 2 to know which machines performed best. Since no scores were provided, I'll reason from first principles and the mission structure, then diversify systematically.

## Reasoning

**Key insight:** We don't know which color (BLUE=clr3 or GREEN=clr2) gives the beneficial item. The scoring says one half decreases score by 1 (good) and the other increases by 1 (bad). We must try both and see which scores lower.

**Core behavior needed:**
1. Go to source (black floor) - specifically to one colored half
2. Return to nest (white floor, toward red wall)
3. Avoid picking up wrong-color items

**State structure analysis:**
- 2-state: GoToBlue→GoToRed wall, triggered by floor color
- 3-state: Explore→GoToColoredHalf→ReturnToNest
- The key transitions: BLACK floor = at source (picked up item), WHITE floor = at nest (delivered)

**Improvements over Round 1:**
- Try higher probabilities on floor transitions (1.0 is deterministic and reliable)
- Try GoAwayColor from the bad half while seeking good half
- Add neighbor-based conditions for robustness
- Try both blue-seeking and green-seeking variants

**LED colors help robots signal state to neighbors - use consistently.**

- WHITE floor = nest area (delivering items)
- GRAY = transit zone
- RED wall = nest side
- BLUE wall (clr=3) = one source half, GREEN wall (clr=2) = other source half
- One color gives -1 score (good), other gives +1 (bad)

**Key insight I've been missing:** The robots need to actually navigate back. GoToColor with RED (clr=1) should work for returning to nest. But I need to make sure the FSM logic is correct:
- When on BLACK floor: robot has just entered source, got an item, now return
- When on WHITE floor: robot delivered, now go get more

**Problem with my previous designs:** In the 3-state FSM with separate "arrived at nest" state, the transition from S0→S2 on WHITE floor means it goes to "return" state when it's already at nest - that's wrong. Let me fix the logic.

**Correct 3-state logic:**
- S0: GoToBlue/Green (seeking source) → on BLACK floor → S1 (return)
- S1: GoToRed (returning to nest) → on WHITE floor → S0 (seek source again)
- Simple 2-state is actually cleaner

**Also trying:** GoAwayColor from the bad half to stay in the good half of source.

**Elitism:** Carrying forward the best designs from rounds 1-2 (both blue and green seekers with p=1.0).

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 8 --vel0 1.0 --clr0 3 --cle0 4 --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 0 --p0x0 1.0 --s1 8 --vel1 1.0 --clr1 1 --cle1 5 --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 2 --p1x0 1.0

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'm not receiving explicit score data, which is unusual. Let me reason carefully about what should work and diversify more aggressively.

## Reasoning

**Critical reflection on my designs so far:**

The basic 2-state FSM (GoToBlue→GoToRed, triggered by floor color) should work in principle. But there may be issues:

1. **Navigation problem**: GoToColor moves toward the NEAREST visible object of that color. The RED wall is on the nest side - robots should navigate toward it to return. BLUE/GREEN walls are on the source side.

2. **Floor color timing**: When a robot enters BLACK floor (source), it immediately gets an item AND the transition fires. The destination state (return) should then navigate back. This seems correct.

3. **Potential issue**: If a robot is already carrying an item and re-enters the source, it replaces the item. We want to avoid the wrong-color source.

4. **New idea**: Try GoAwayColor from RED wall when seeking source (pushes robots away from nest toward source). Combined with GoToRed when returning.

5. **Another idea**: Use GRAY floor detection - when transitioning from source back, detect gray = in transit, which can help with intermediate states.

6. **Important**: I've been assuming clr=1 is RED. Let me verify: clr {1,2,3,4,5,6} - the problem says wall blocks are BLUE and GREEN, nest wall is RED. Standard colors: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan. This seems right.

7. **GoAwayRed** (clr=1) when seeking source might be more effective than GoToBlue/Green because it directly pushes away from nest.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I'm not receiving score data between rounds, which means I need to reason purely from first principles and be more systematic.

## Reasoning

**Deep analysis of what could be failing:**

1. The basic 2-state FSM (GoToColor→ReturnToRed) should work IF the color perceptions are correct. But I realize: the source wall blocks are BLUE and GREEN, but what if the robots can't see the wall colors from the source area because they're AT the black floor and the wall is behind them?

2. **Critical issue**: When on BLACK floor (source), the robot may not see the colored walls because it's already past them. The colored walls are BEHIND the source area. So GoToBlue might navigate robots TO the source, but once there, they're on black floor and transition fires. This is actually correct behavior.

3. **The real problem might be**: I don't know which color (blue=clr3 or green=clr2) is the GOOD one. I've been trying both but without feedback I can't tell which scored better.

4. **New approach**: Try machines that AVOID one color while seeking the other, to ensure robots only enter the correct half. Use GoAwayColor from the BAD half.

5. **Try exploration-based approaches** that rely more on random walks and floor color detection rather than wall color targeting.

6. **Also try**: GoToColor RED for returning (nest wall), and GoAwayColor RED for going to source (away from nest).

7. **Radical new idea**: Use neighbor-based signaling - robots at source signal with LEDs, others follow.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I've been getting no score feedback, which means I need to reason more carefully and try fundamentally different approaches.

## Reasoning

**Fundamental reassessment:**

The key challenge is I don't know which wall color (BLUE=clr3 or GREEN=clr2) gives the GOOD item (score -1). Without feedback, I must hedge by trying both systematically.

**What I know works in principle:**
- GoToColor(blue/green) navigates toward source half
- GoToColor(red) navigates toward nest
- Floor BLACK = at source, WHITE = at nest

**What might be going wrong:**
1. The wall colors might not be visible from far away - robots starting in the middle might not see walls
2. GoAwayRed (clr=1) might be better for finding source since it's always visible from anywhere
3. Maybe the problem is robots oscillate near walls instead of traversing properly

**New strategies to try:**
1. **Exploration-based**: Random walk until BLACK floor, then GoToRed to return. Simple and robust.
2. **GoAwayRed to source**: Pushes robots away from nest toward source, then GoToRed to return.
3. **Gray floor as intermediate trigger**: Use GRAY detection to start orienting toward source/nest.
4. **Combine floor+color**: Use floor color as primary trigger, wall color as secondary direction aid.

**Critical insight I may have missed**: When robots start in the center on GRAY floor, GoToColor(blue) or GoToColor(green) might not work if the walls aren't visible. Exploration (random walk) until BLACK floor is detected might be more reliable for reaching the source.

# ROUND 8/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

I've been running 7 rounds without score feedback. Let me completely rethink.

**What I know for certain:**
- Score is MINIMISED, so delivering BLUE-half items or GREEN-half items matters
- One color gives -1 (good), one gives +1 (bad)
- I've been trying both colors in parallel but without knowing which is better

**Fundamental problems with my designs:**

1. **The 2-state FSM logic may be broken**: `S0: GoToBlue → on BLACK floor → S1: GoToRed → on WHITE floor → S0`. This SHOULD work. When robot reaches source (BLACK), it transitions to return state. When it reaches nest (WHITE), it transitions back to seek source.

2. **BUT**: If the robot starts on GRAY floor, and S0 fires on BLACK floor to go to S1, the robot in S1 (GoToRed) will stay in S1 until WHITE floor. This is correct. The cycle should work.

3. **Possible issue**: Robots starting on GRAY might not be able to see RED/BLUE/GREEN walls from center of arena. If the arena is large enough, walls aren't visible. In that case, GoToColor does nothing useful (no target seen = random-ish movement).

4. **New hypothesis**: Maybe I should use GoAwayColor(RED) to push robots toward source since RED is on the nest side and always visible from center. This would be more reliable than GoToBlue/Green.

5. **Key question**: What if score has been very similar between blue-seeking and green-seeking designs? I should try machines that EXPLICITLY avoid the bad half by using GoAway from it.

6. **Trying a completely different structure**: Use GRAY floor as trigger to start navigating (when in transit), use wall visibility (C=7) to steer toward correct color.

**Most promising approach**: 
- Explore randomly → detect BLACK floor → return to RED
- This avoids any assumption about which source color is good, just picks up whatever item is there and delivers it... wait, but that doesn't distinguish between good and bad items!

**Critical insight**: We MUST target one specific color half. The random walk approach will pick up equal amounts of good and bad items, netting to ~0. We need to specifically go to one half only.

**Strategy**: Pair GoToColor(good_half) with GoAwayColor(bad_half) to stay in the correct zone. Try both blue-good and green-good variants. Also try using neighbor perception to aggregate robots at source.

# ROUND 9/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

After 8 rounds without explicit score feedback, I need to radically rethink my approach.

**Core problem analysis:**

The mission says "an item from one half decreases score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1." To minimize score, we want robots to ONLY deliver items from the GOOD half (the one that decreases score by 1).

**Why my designs might all be scoring ~0:**
- If robots visit both source halves equally, score ≈ 0
- If GoToColor works, blue-seekers should score negative (if blue is good) or positive (if bad)
- Without feedback, I don't know which is better

**New critical insight:** Maybe I should try machines that actively AVOID one half using GoAwayColor while the return state uses GoToRed. The avoidance creates spatial bias toward the other half.

**What if both blue AND green targeted machines score similarly (both near 0 or both negative)?** Then maybe the issue is throughput - robots aren't completing enough round trips.

**Maximize throughput approach:**
- Make transitions fire reliably (p=1.0) ✓ already doing this
- Use faster/more direct navigation
- Minimize time spent in wrong zones

**New idea - use GRAY floor trigger:**
When returning robot hits GRAY (leaving source), it could start GoToRed more aggressively. When seeking robot hits GRAY (leaving nest), start GoToBlue/Green.

**Also:** Try machines where S0 starts with GoToBlue/Green from the beginning (robots start on GRAY, so they'll immediately navigate toward source) with transition on BLACK, then return. The WHITE→BLACK cycle should be fast.

**Most important insight I haven't tried:** What if I use the LED colors of neighbors to coordinate? Robots in "return" state (magenta LED) vs "seek" state (yellow LED) - other robots could follow yellow-LED robots toward source or magenta-LED robots toward nest... but C=7 only detects wall/floor colors, not robot LEDs. So this won't work directly.

**Trying completely different: use GRAY floor as the key trigger for direction changes** rather than BLACK/WHITE only. When on GRAY going toward source, transition to source-seeking. When on GRAY going toward nest, keep going to nest.

Actually, the simplest reliable design remains the 2-state with p=1.0. Let me focus on ensuring I have both blue AND green variants, and add some that use GRAY as intermediate trigger.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

This is the final round. I need to make my best possible machines.

**After 9 rounds of trying both blue-seeking and green-seeking designs without score feedback, I must accept uncertainty about which color is "good." My final population should:**

1. Include strong variants of BOTH blue-seeking and green-seeking (cover both possibilities)
2. Focus on the most reliable designs: simple 2-state with p=1.0 transitions
3. Include some 3-state variants that are more robust
4. Maximize throughput (fast round trips)

**What I'm most confident about:**
- The 2-state design: GoToColor(X) → on BLACK floor → GoToColor(RED) → on WHITE floor → GoToColor(X) is the most logical and reliable foraging controller
- p=1.0 transitions are most reliable
- Both blue (clr=3) and green (clr=2) variants needed

**Final strategy - 12 machines:**
- 2x core blue-seeker (2-state, p=1.0) - ELITISM
- 2x core green-seeker (2-state, p=1.0) - ELITISM  
- 2x GoAwayRed variants (push toward source, return via GoToRed)
- 2x 3-state with GoToColor + avoidance of bad half
- 2x 3-state with GoAwayRed + GoToBlue/Green + GoToRed
- 2x 4-state comprehensive variants

The key insight for the final round: maximize the chance that at least one design is optimal for whichever color is the "good" one, and ensure high throughput.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
